## Round 3 resumption — 2026-11-09
- Model: GPT-6 Astra (`omnigateway/gpt-6-astra`), resumed after reported `opus-pr` credential failure. Actual base HEAD: `da8f6b87`.
- Preserved partial uncommitted writer epoch implementation, writer regressions and contention benchmark. Preserved unrelated bun.lock and three user specs; stash untouched.
- Binding corrections: publication order cannot be inferred from sequence assignment; preempted producers must be accounted for. Barrier admission must remain asynchronously timeout-bounded, producers must not wait. Existing partial epoch protocol is provisional.
- Limiter must preserve conservative history across all configuration transitions, including concurrency-only history; fractional 60-second windows require 61 one-second buckets, with conservative bucket-end expiration and backward-clock handling.
- Boot entropy must seed the actual request-ID RNG, not merely probe the OS. All implementation and benchmark results remain provisional pending independent review.

Task 2: review (gpt-6-astra) — Needs fixes: 5 Important, 1 Minor
Ruling: "breaker transitions batched" — the spec makes transitions immediate because other replicas route on them; in single mode routing reads the in-process snapshot, and the final DB row equals TS's. Keep batched for slice 1, but the fix must split the event API into a measurement event and a transition event, so the cluster slice can route transitions immediately without an API change — cost if wrong: a cluster-slice rework of the health path.
Ruling: "one-batch loss bound" — bounded memory is binding, so a stalled DB must queue. Fix the deadline so it is measured from the oldest unwritten event's enqueue time (not from dequeue). Document that loss beyond one batch can happen only while the DB is stalled — cost if wrong: a larger loss window on a hard kill during a DB stall.
Ruling: diagnostics use the `tracing` crate (the gateway installs a TS-format subscriber in Task 5); the writer emits a rate-limited warn for drops and an error for failed batches — cost if wrong: a later logging swap.

Ruling: lone surrogates — Rust replaces them with U+FFFD where TS re-escapes them as \udXXX. Only malformed client input can contain one. A provider cannot tell the gateway did it (the client's bytes were already invalid), and the only cost is a prompt-cache-key mismatch for that input. Carrying WTF-16 strings through the IR would cost every request. Accepted as a documented difference; the spec's contract line gets an exception note — cost if wrong: one malformed-input class has a different cache key.
Ruling: usage pass-through of non-numeric counts — only a malformed upstream sends them; Rust coerces to numbers the way TS's arithmetic sites do. Accepted as a documented difference — cost if wrong: a malformed upstream's string count shows as a number or 0 instead of a string.
Ruling: Retry-After loose JS date forms (no timezone, free-form) — JS Date.parse leniency is host-dependent; Rust reads IMF/RFC 850/asctime plus ISO-8601 as UTC. Accepted, documented — cost if wrong: a provider sending a non-standard date gets no retry-after hint.

Ruling: the Rust gateway does NOT auto-load `.env`. It matches the shipped compiled binary (scripts/build-binary.ts sets autoloadDotenv:false, which is the Docker/k8s path). `omni start`/`omni service` already pass the root `.env` explicitly (the CLI parses it; systemd uses EnvironmentFile=). Only the npm `bun gateway.js` and dev `bun apps/gateway/src/index.ts` paths auto-load, and npm is dropped by the spec. Keep dotenv.rs (Bun-semantics loader) for a later CLI `omni start --foreground` parity check if it proves needed; otherwise remove it in fix round 2 — implementer's call, recorded in the report. The spec's "Rust must load .env explicitly" line is superseded by this ruling — cost if wrong: an operator who runs the bare binary from a directory with .env, relying on Bun autoload, gets "OMNI_ENCRYPTION_KEY must be set" and must export the vars or use `omni start`.

Task 4: round-1 re-review dispatched in parallel with fix round 2 (read-only, range e9cd83b2..db0ea2b5)
Task 4: round-1 re-review (ses_e468617b3ffeytaw5BY1Q13fDh) — finding 1 (invalid-key reads) ADDRESSED; finding 2 (seed race) NOT ADDRESSED: pending overflow drops debits; 10 s grace can double-count; seeded_ids unbounded; the deadline comes from job time, not install time. Minor: the "within one refresh" test allows 10 intervals.
Ruling: replace time-based dedupe with commit-ordered accounting. TS gets exactness by ordering, not bookkeeping: rateLimit.ts:531-534 writes the row first, then debits, so an unseeded key ignores the debit and the seed reads the row. Rust reproduces that ordering. The single writer task applies each completion's limiter debit only after its batch commits (post-commit callback/channel to the limiter, in commit order). Seeds run on the writer's own connection between batches (a request on the writer's channel), so a seed's aggregate always includes exactly the rows committed before it, and every later debit lands after install. Remove the pending queue, seeded_ids, grace window and request-id reconciliation. This also covers queued item 9 (two-statement read): the seed runs between batches on the only writing connection. Cost if wrong: a debit lags its completion by ≤1 batch (~200 ms), so a 5h/1w limit can overshoot by the requests completed in that 200 ms; that matches TS's own "row first, then debit" lag, plus batching.
Task 4: fix round 2 done — commits 45aaef61, e9a371c0 (db0ea2b5..e9a371c0); re-review split into A (limiter/seed/writer/buckets) and B (rest)

Task 4: fix round 2/5 (B 7/7 addressed; A 2 addressed, 1 open + 1 new)
Ruling: seed delivery must never block the writer. A full sink makes the writer drop the seed result and count+warn. The limiter treats a seed that never arrives the same as a failed seed: the window stays unseeded (debits for it are ignored, as TS does) and the seeder retries after backoff. Ordering is preserved because a dropped seed installs nothing, and every debit after it is ignored until a later seed's aggregate includes those rows — cost if wrong: under a sustained full sink, long-window limits stay unenforced (fail open) until the limiter drains; same direction as TS's fail-open on coord faults.
Ruling: bound the seed payload in SQL — aggregate per grain bucket in the query (GROUP BY bucket start, as TS sumBuckets does), plus the exact-reset stamps capped with ORDER BY at LIMIT (stamp cap) inside the query, never row ids — cost if wrong: the exact 429 reset falls back to bucket granularity beyond the stamp cap (TS uses bucket granularity anyway).

Ruling: nesting depth — keep serde_json's 128-level limit (it makes the stack-overflow concern from the anthropic review impossible on the text path). Document it as an accepted difference: a client body nested >128 levels deep gets the same "invalid JSON" error (INTERNAL 500 in TS terms → mapped in 5b) instead of being processed. No real agent request nests that deep; TS has no request-size limit at all, and the spec adds one. Public parse fns that take a preconstructed Value document that the caller must have parsed with the bounded parser — cost if wrong: an absurdly nested but valid body is refused.

Task 5a: openai+responses review (ses_e4598b501ffetTaTSWR9dABPkW) — files fully compared, closely aligned. 1 Important: embedded tool-argument JSON strings use default serde_json depth 128; TS JSON.parse accepts e.g. 150 nested arrays → Chat silently substitutes {} and Responses returns BAD_REQUEST where TS keeps the value. Gaps: n>1, legacy functions/function_call, response_format json_object, unicode call ids at the 64-UTF-16 boundary.
Ruling (supersedes the earlier 128-level ruling): one depth bound D for the outer body and for embedded argument strings, chosen to cover TS's practical limit. The implementer measures the depth at which Bun 1.4.4's JSON.stringify (the TS gateway re-serializes upstream) throws RangeError, and what the TS gateway then returns to the client. Set D ≥ that depth, and make Rust stack-safe at D (measure; set the tokio worker thread_stack_size if needed; every recursive path — serde parse, clone, zod port, jsjson writer — must survive D). Beyond D, return deterministically the same error TS returns past its limit. Never a silent {} for valid JSON that is too deep. Boundary vectors on both sides — cost if wrong: bodies near the limit behave differently from TS, or a larger worker stack.
Task 5a: egress review (ses_e4598b4edffevo61NNyqPBASqJ) — all three renderers closely match TS (stop reasons, tool ids, thinking, post-commit errors, Responses lifecycle + sequence numbers); SSE framing matches proxy.ts; header names/formatting OK; 429-only Retry-After OK. 1 Important: streaming allocations beyond the frame (anthropic native delta clones; responses item objects, deep-cloned snapshots, native reasoning strings); reuse-buffer test can't prove the allocation claim. Minors: reset header omission vs TS throw (known); usage-on-finish-chunk matches frozen TS (not a defect); missing vectors: native __proto__ merge, non-string reasoning coercion, native delta type collision, non-finite header values.
Ruling: the allocation finding is narrowed to per-chunk work. The spec says "no per-chunk allocation beyond the encoded frame". Per-DELTA paths (text, arguments and reasoning deltas, the hot path, ~3 µs/chunk in TS) must write borrowed data straight into the buffer with no clones. Per-ITEM and terminal work (Responses output_item.done snapshots and the final response.completed output, which TS also builds as objects) may allocate once per item or stream. Remove the per-delta clones (anthropic native delta clone; any per-delta string/object in responses); a deep clone at item close is acceptable if it is the only copy (move instead of clone where possible). Verification: a counting global allocator in one test binary (stable Rust, #[global_allocator] wrapping System — this needs no unsafe in our code? GlobalAlloc impl requires `unsafe impl`; the workspace forbids unsafe_code) → RULING: no allocation-counting allocator (forbid(unsafe_code) blocks it). Instead (a) a structural test that a long text/arguments delta sequence does not grow buffer capacity after warm-up beyond the frame size, and (b) the Task 6 benchmark's many-chunks CPU/req is the real check against the spec target (≤1.5 ms/req for 2000 chunks) — cost if wrong: a hidden per-delta allocation shows up only as a missed benchmark target.
Ruling: reset header for a non-representable instant — keep omitting the header (fail-safe) instead of failing the response the way TS throws. Real headroom values can't reach it; documented difference — cost if wrong: none observable.

Task 5a: round-1 re-review ingress+IR (ses_e45544638ffe545UFky7KfIIzo) — discriminants ADDRESSED; depth NOT ADDRESSED: bare deep Value clones dropped recursively at anthropic_tools.rs:282 (parse_custom_tool), responses.rs:170 (read_tool_output), responses.rs:278 (raw.type on rejection); JsValue::from + JsValue clone/drop recursive; outer-body depth tests don't cover Responses. Client-triggerable stack overflow (Important).
Ruling: stack safety must hold structurally, not by auditing call sites. Run ALL client-JSON processing (parse → validate → IR → wire body serialization) inside `stacker::maybe_grow` (red zone ≥ 256 KiB, grow segment sized so D=40,000 fits, measured) at the single ingress entry point that 5b calls, and drop the client Value/IR inside that same guarded scope (or hand the result to a type whose Drop is iterative). Per-call-site deep::Owned stays where it already is, but correctness no longer depends on it. Tests: a 2 MiB-stack thread runs the public entry point for each dialect with a D-deep payload at every client-JSON position the reviewer named (custom tool input_schema, Responses function_call_output, raw.type, outer body, argument strings), on valid and rejected paths — cost if wrong: a deep body needs a grown stack segment (heap) per request; rare and bounded by D and the body-size limit.
Task 5a: round-1 re-review egress+vectors (ses_e45544634ffe6fP8dl4mtGfEmQ) — byte oracle ADDRESSED (spot-checked 1e+21; regen cmp equal); coverage additions ADDRESSED; report accuracy ADDRESSED; allocations NOT ADDRESSED: responses.rs:179 to_string on non-string native reasoning; :530 native merge deltas clone keys+values; :479/489/499 text/reasoning/argument deltas append to the retained item string; anthropic.rs:172 write_spread builds key-order Vecs on numeric-key objects (jsjson.rs:141,164-166,518).
Ruling: allocation rule refined. (a) Appending a delta to the per-item accumulated text that Responses must emit in output_text.done/response.completed is REQUIRED state (TS holds it too). Amortized String growth is allowed, so not a defect. (b) Native merge delta deep clones (responses.rs:530) ARE a defect: merge by moving the owned delta values into the retained native item (the delta is owned and consumed), and serialize the frame from borrowed data. (c) js::to_string on a non-string native reasoning value (responses.rs:179) is a rare malformed-upstream path → allowed (document it). (d) jsjson key-order Vecs: only when an object has array-index-like keys (V8 hoisting). The common path must not allocate — confirm that the no-numeric-key path builds no Vec, and allow the Vec on the numeric-key path (rare) — cost if wrong: a few rare allocations, which the Task 6 benchmark would show.

Ruling: shallow requests must not grow the stack. Implementer picks one: (a) the stacker idiom — maybe_grow with a small red zone (≤256 KiB) and a modest segment at EVERY recursive function on client-JSON paths (serde_stacker already does this for parse), never once at the top; or (b) an iterative depth pre-scan of the raw body and of each embedded argument string (bracket counting outside strings), growing only when depth exceeds a threshold measured safe on 2 MiB in debug. Required test: a shallow request through ingest takes the no-grow path (a thread-local counter on the grow branch in deep.rs), and the D-deep tests still pass on 2 MiB — cost if wrong: either a per-call check cost (a) or one extra O(n) scan per body (b); both are small next to the 16 MiB mmap.

Task 5b: review support+e2e (ses_e447a7223ffeYcRU6e1W1DM0j0) — jsc_json is a JSC-diagnostics parser (not a duplicate serializer), iterative ✅; logger format ✅; matrix complete ✅. Important: (L1) logging.rs:196 emits fields outside the TS allowlist; (E1) concurrency row timing-based, no handshake, no assert that the 2nd is 429; (E2) disconnect row accepts EOF before the delta and discards the body; (E3) bytes compared as UTF-8 strings (res.text, String) — use byte buffers; (E4) global regex masks too broad, timed headers replaced wholesale. Minors: jsc_json astral/UTF-16 diag differences; DB compare uses projections not full rows; equality alone doesn't prove the scenario happened.
Ruling: D1 — TS's pending[] is unbounded too, but bounded memory is binding. Cap pre-commit buffering at 1 MiB of encoded event bytes or 4096 events; past the cap, treat it as committed (flush and stream). Do not fail it: TS would eventually stream it, and committing early only loses the ability to fail over — cost if wrong: a pathological upstream that sends >1 MiB of non-delta events and then fails can't fail over.
Ruling: S1 — read idle timeout 255 s per read, matching Bun's idleTimeout (body and between requests on keep-alive), plus header-read timeout 255 s; no total body deadline (TS has none).
Ruling: S5 — the reviewer's "both headers" note is not a finding: TS accepts both when the tokens match, and Rust already does (Task 4 api_key_header; e2e "both headers" row). The repeated-header case IS a gap: join repeated values with ", " the way WHATWG Headers.get does, then apply the TS rule.
Ruling: E2 — the disconnect row must prove a mid-stream disconnect: gate the upstream (it waits on a release signal after the first delta), read until the first delta, abort, then release; assert exactly one 499 row in each DB.
Ruling: DB compare — use full rows (SELECT *) with an explicit, documented list of excluded timing columns; any new column is compared by default.

Ruling: L1 allowlist side effect — Rust crates' snake_case diagnostic fields (api_key_id, error, dropped_total) are dropped. Map snake_case onto TS LogFields names (api_key_id → apiKeyId, etc.) before filtering; a field with no TS equivalent is dropped only if TS has nowhere to put it. If a TS warn carries the same information under a different field (e.g. `reason`), put it there. Goes into the re-review findings — cost if wrong: Rust operators lose writer/limiter diagnostics.

Task 6: hang debug (ses_e43868b56ffedCbhx0a5Ht8eA1) — root cause: hyper 1.10.1 regression (#4143) left a chunked terminator unflushed ~1 in 1M streamed responses; one keep-alive client wedged and oha -w waited forever. Evidence: live capture showed the gateway idle, /health 0.7 ms, one connection at the head of response 2079 with no body. Fix: hyper ≥1.11.1 (locked 1.12.0), deterministic test http1_flush.rs (RED on 1.10.1). Per-event WARN flood removed (drop_warnings.rs). Commits ba2147e4 437c4ef5. 3 bench runs clean, e2e equal, 441 tests.
Ruling: the writer shedding most rows at bench rates (3.7–3.9 M events dropped per full run; request_logs incomplete; long-window limits under-count because debits come from committed rows) is NOT acceptable as steady state. The spec's shed path is for real overload (DB stalled), not normal operation at the gateway's own throughput. Required: writer capacity ≥ the gateway's max sustained throughput on the same 4 cores, with zero drops in every bench scenario. Coalesce Begin+Finish that land in the same batch into one final upsert (allowed by the Task 2 ruling), measure, then go further if needed (batch size, prepared multi-row statements, rollup aggregation cost, writer thread placement). If capacity still can't keep up, bring the measurement back — no shedding during normal load — cost if wrong: lower peak req/s, which is the honest number.
Ruling: report only zero-drop throughput as the headline. The bench must report writer drops per scenario (read the gateway's shutdown/drop counters or count rows vs requests) so a shedding run can't pass as fast.
Ruling: add -t (per-request timeout) to non-held bench scenarios so a wedged connection becomes a counted error, not a hang (scripts/bench-gateway.ts is ours, not frozen TS).
Ruling: throughput is bimodal (chat-stream 49 k vs 26 k req/s, same binary, same quiet host). Find the cause before publishing numbers (CPU frequency, P/E cores under taskset 0-3, writer shedding, thermal). Publish median-of-3 with min/max.

Bench now reports drops; -t on non-held; vCPU calibration (all vCPUs ±2%). Bimodality cause: a headless Chrome in the same VM (software WebGL, 4.3–5.5 cores), not vCPU placement. Published median-of-3 capped at 20k req/s, 0 drops: chat p99 2.12, agent-100k 0.610, many-chunks 1.154, overhead p99 0.25, held 82 MiB (miss). Withdrew the 51k figures (shedding).

OPERATOR DECISION (Nov 9): writer capacity → option 1, time-ordered request ids.
Ruling (implementing the decision): request id = `req_` + RFC 9562 UUIDv7 (48-bit ms timestamp + random; version nibble 7, variant 10), minted with a monotonic generator within the process so ids from one process sort by mint order. Format stays `req_<uuid>`; only the version nibble and the time prefix change, so every derived id (resp_req_…, msg_req_…_n, Grok x-grok-req-id, Antigravity/OpenAI/Qoder derivations) remains well-formed. Upstream exposure: providers already see a fresh id per request; v7 additionally reveals the mint time, which the request itself already carries. Documented as a deliberate difference from TS (uuid v4) in the spec, the bench doc and the cutover notes. The e2e masks must accept v4 (TS) and v7 (Rust) at the same paths, and the conformance driver asserts that Rust ids are v7 with a timestamp within ±N s of the request. Then re-run the writer capacity measurement and the uncapped bench: the goal is zero drops at the gateway's full throughput on the same 4 cores. If that still isn't reached, bring the measurement back.

Task 6: round-1 re-review (ses_e409783a6ffeCfZ9U5b93zrcFJ) — P1 P3 W1/W2 W3 W4 ✅ (endpoint off by default, env read once, disabled POST = ordinary NOT_FOUND like TS); P2 NOT ADDRESSED: no whole-body run for multi-chunk bodies >4096 bytes. New Important: (B1) the barrier awaits queue admission and completion with no server deadline or concurrency limit, so concurrent callers can starve try_send traffic and induce drops; (B2) barrier counters are not a coherent snapshot (enqueued can include post-barrier events; publication precedes the enqueue-counter increment).
Ruling: B1/B2 are bench-only (OMNI_BENCH=1) but must still be correct, since they produce the published zero-drop evidence. Bound the barrier: one concurrent barrier (a second call gets 409), a server-side timeout (e.g. 30 s → 504 inconclusive), and admission via the same try_send as other events (a barrier refused for a full queue → inconclusive, never blocking). Counters: the writer stamps a sequence number on each accepted event at enqueue (one atomic increment before publication); the barrier records the sequence at its own admission and returns committed/failed/dropped for events ≤ that watermark. P2: add a whole-body run for every multi-chunk vector regardless of size.

Task 6: v7 done — c23596ed 394287df c50977ff. Uncapped median-of-3: chat-stream 55,770 req/s (55,035–56,385), p99 3.05; 0 drops/failed/missing/pending in every scenario ×3; writer one core: v7 119.6k events/s (0 dropped) vs v4 78.5k (273k dropped). held-streams 101 MiB (MISS, worse: the rate-limited-key 1m window holds ~1.2M stamps at 51k req/s; the allocator retains the pages). Same-ms ids sequential → per-ms count leak.
Ruling: switch to RFC 9562 method 3 (a random increment within the same ms, ≥ 1 and < 2^32 of rand_b space, preserving monotonicity) — closes the count leak at no throughput cost — cost if wrong: none (ids stay unique and sorted).
Ruling: rate-limit 1m request window memory must not scale with request rate. TS keeps one timestamp per request in the ring (memoryCoord window) too, so per-request memory is a TS trait, but held-streams ≤80 MiB is a binding target. Bucket the 1m window in fixed sub-windows (e.g. 1 s × 60 counters per subject), but only if headroom/reset/429 parity vectors stay exact. If exactness requires per-request stamps (TS's exact reset = the oldest stamp + 60 s), keep exact stamps up to the limit+1 per subject (a window can never need more than `limit` stamps to decide admission or reset) and drop older ones — bounded by the configured limit, not the request rate. Measure held-streams RSS in the full run.
Task 6: fix round 2 done — 823f2c66 (P2 whole-body) 91e7380f (v7 method 3 random increment) d190ab0e (barrier: one at a time 409, 30 s 504, try_send 503; sequence watermark, coherent prefix) ca80bcfa (1m ring capped at the limit and at 65,536; bucket fallback above) b1c22374 (docs). Task 6: high review writer+barrier+bench (ses_e40426ac6ffe7l4lq02GFIDXKY) — gating ✅, single-flight/503/cancel cleanup ✅, limiter try_send ✅, harness ✅, 96 metric summaries recomputed from the json all match, md/json spot-checks match. Important: (H1) a rejected Seed increments dropped without a sequence → every later barrier prefix incoherent; (H2) the per-event RwLock::read can block behind barrier admission (the request path must never wait), and flush()'s synchronous .write() is not bounded by the 30 s timeout; (H3) withdrawn shedding numbers still published (md history tables, json previous.shedding). Minor: calibration claims contradict the raw json; the targets table lacks explicit deferred rows and its CLI figure is stale (99.3 ms); queue headroom ≈1.18 s at 55k, not ~3 s.
Ruling: H2 — the request path must be lock-free. Replace the RwLock with an epoch/ticket protocol: producers do one fetch_add on a sequence counter BEFORE try_send, and the barrier is just another try_send'd event carrying no watermark of its own. The writer derives the watermark from the channel's FIFO: every event dequeued before the barrier has a sequence assigned before the barrier's (FIFO ⇒ published earlier). Events assigned but whose try_send failed bump a dropped counter tagged with their sequence. To be exact, the barrier records the counter value at its own fetch_add and the writer reports committed+failed for events dequeued before it, plus dropped events with seq < watermark — tracked in a small fixed array (or a sharded counter) keyed by the barrier epoch. If that cannot be made exact without waiting, choose exactness for the bench (off by default) and lock-freedom for production by compiling the barrier path only under OMNI_BENCH (a runtime check before any lock is taken; production takes no lock at all). Seeds stay outside barrier accounting entirely (H1).
Ruling: H3 — remove withdrawn numbers from the published md/json; keep the history in the sdd report only.
Task 6: high review ids+limiter (ses_e40426ad1ffefPbXdabOnHKKi2) — UUIDv7 layout, process-global ordering, backward-clock, exhaustion ✅; method 3 ✅ (hides exact counts, not statistical estimates); derived ids and masks ✅; fixed-limit (≤65,536) exactness proven and TS parity test passes. Important: (L1) lowering then raising a limit can make Rust ADMIT where TS refuses (repro: limit 3 at 1/2/3 s, lower to 1, raise to 2 → Rust admits, TS refuses); a concurrency-only subject keeps no minute history; (L2) the bucket fallback merges oldest-first with no width bound — a repro overcounts 480k vs a true 240k after 120 s. Minor: the method-3 privacy claim is overstated; the long-window seed keeps the EARLIEST capped rows while debits evict the oldest, so exact resets are lost after truncation (safe side); rand::random panics if OS entropy fails; the 48-bit timestamp boundary.
Ruling: L1 — Rust must never admit what TS would refuse. Keep the ring capacity tied to the HIGHEST limit seen for that subject within the last window (and keep recording for any subject that has any limit configured, minute-window or not), so lowering then raising a limit never discards stamps TS would still count. Memory stays bounded by max(limit over the last minute, capped at 65,536). Past the cap, fall back per L2.
Ruling: L2 — the fallback uses fixed-width time buckets (1 s × 60 per subject) holding counts; the count is exact to within one bucket at the trailing edge (overcount ≤ one bucket's count, never undercount); the reset is the start of the oldest non-empty bucket + 60 s (≤ 1 s later than exact). Document the bound; test it with the reviewer's repro (quarter-ms stamps over 120 s).
Ruling: method-3 docs — state "hides exact intervening counts; permits statistical rate estimates". Seed truncation: keep the NEWEST `cap` stamps (ORDER BY at DESC LIMIT cap), matching the debit eviction. Entropy: use the fallible OsRng path once at startup; if it fails, the gateway refuses to boot with a clear error (it can't mint keys or ids safely anyway).

Ruling (D3 refusal-path store read): NOT allowed. Spec: "touches Postgres never" on the request path; single-node already reads the exact reset from in-memory stamps (omni-gateway-core ratelimit.rs:32). Cluster refusals must compute reset from Redis-held data (e.g. oldest stamp in the bucket hash / window ZSET) or, if exact oldestSince isn't recoverable from Redis, report the conservative later reset (bucket start + window) and document it as a safe-side difference, like single-node's capped ring. Cost if wrong: Retry-After can be up to one bucket width later than TS's exact value.
Rulings on D3 other differences: (a) no anonymous gauge slot without a concurrency ceiling — accept (spec rule, decisions proven equal). (b) local fallback does not enforce 5h/1w — accept for outage fail-open (spec: fail open with local limiter; long windows unseeded locally), document. (c) new omni:load:* keys — accept: spec forbids mixed TS/Rust fleets (Recreate rollout), document in cutover runbook.

Ruling I1/I2: make seeding a single atomic Lua operation that installs counts + ranges + a seed generation/watermark, and make debits carry the row timestamp so the debit script skips rows at or before the seed's watermark (TS-equivalent: seed = committed rows up to watermark; later debits add). This also closes the requeue double count. If a TS-unchanged BUCKETS_SEED must be kept for compat, add a Rust-only companion that is atomic with it. Cost if wrong: a new hash field TS ignores.
Ruling I5: preserve per-debit float arithmetic order (apply each cost in Lua in sequence within the batch script, or split) — exact TS state is the contract.

Ruling R1: skip only debits whose ROW the seed actually included — store the seed's row-selection boundary (since_ms) per window and skip a debit iff commit visible AND row_at >= boundary; keep per-row association when a transaction spans the boundary. Tokenless fallback: retry lock acquisition before taking the snapshot; never install with an empty token while debits could be ignored (or make debits wait on an in-progress marker). Cost if wrong: one extra seed attempt under lock contention.

E5c blocker (ses_e2b4ab571ffe6k4k6Ma2RGHM3p, investigation commit 2326768a): frozen TS `setup claude|opencode` cannot complete on Bun 1.4.4 — apps/cli/src/prompt.ts readLine() returns out of `for await (… of process.stdin)`, which destroys stdin, so the second of five mapping prompts exits 1 "The operation was aborted", no file written (8 real-CLI reproductions + 3 repeats). An earlier 96-case "pass" was invalid (both sides exited before prompting) and is not evidence.
Ruling: Rust implements the intended five-question flow and does NOT reproduce the abort; documented divergence "interactive multi-prompt commands complete in Rust where frozen TS aborts after the first answer". Oracle for content/output/exit codes = frozen TS through its own seam (apps/cli/src/run.ts `options.prompt` injection, real generators in @omni/control, real setupFs), not the broken stdin reader; prompt strings and order taken from frozen setup.ts and asserted byte-exact on a PTY against Rust alone. The first prompt and every pre-prompt failure stay byte-identical to the real TS CLI. Same rule applies to any other multi-prompt command (e.g. set-password confirmation) if it hits the same stream destruction; single-prompt behaviour stays identical. Cost if wrong: Rust `omni setup` succeeds where TS failed — a script relying on the failure is not a plausible consumer.

Controller read of crates/omni-cli/src/password.rs: (a) hidden input shells out to `stty` found on PATH; (b) backspace pops one byte, so backspacing over a multi-byte character (e.g. "é") leaves a partial sequence that from_utf8_lossy turns into U+FFFD inside the password — TS removes one UTF-16 code unit of the decoded line. Ruling (B1 expiry carrier): the full TS-exact expiry representation (SQLite stores 1.5 as REAL, NaN as NULL, ±Infinity as REAL) needs Option<f64> through store, snapshot and router — parked as roadmap row X2, not done in this branch. Within the existing Option<i64> carrier the non-finite cases must still route as TS does: NaN → NULL (no expiry), never 0 (0 would make every request refresh); +Infinity saturates to "never expires"; −Infinity to "always expired"; fractional truncates (sub-millisecond difference, documented). Cost if wrong: only a malformed token-endpoint `expires_in` reaches these paths; real providers send integer seconds. X2 also owns: Rust reading a TS-written REAL expires_at must not drop the credential.

New Important R1: I2's fix admits ~40k-deep events, and the emitted StreamEvent/ContentBlockStart/Delta values leave the grown-stack scope and are dropped/cloned recursively on a normal worker stack (queue drop on cancellation, emitted event drop) → stack overflow, process-fatal, for accepted input; source-established, not crash-probed by the reviewer. New minors: (1) profile.rs iterates std::env::vars(), which panics at startup on any non-Unicode env entry, and retains every OMNI_* value; (2) deep-carrier detection walks tool/vendor JSON on every encode with a boxed iterator per container; (3) each SSE event is depth-scanned twice and always parsed through serde_stacker. Ruling: codec fix round 2 = R1 (stack-safe ownership of emitted events incl. queue cancellation; low-stack subprocess regressions from the reviewer's two repros) + minor 1 (vars_os, keep only the profile override keys; a startup crash on an unrelated env var is a regression) + minor 3 (single depth scan, shallow path via parse_bounded). Minor 2 is not fixed in this round: the traversal helper is shared with the already-reviewed custom codec, and the cost is unmeasured; recorded as a follow-up to measure with an Anthropic tool-bearing encode micro-benchmark before cutover. B1 refresh review (ses_e2b0c6939ffetLQw5cpBmyX3vd, b1-refresh-review.md): changes required, no Critical. I1 snapshot publication not monotonic: the poller's full rebuild does an unconditional store(next) and can overwrite a completed refresh/disable patch (stale token or re-enabled credential until the next poll); equal (token_version, updated_at) lets an enabled row re-enable a disabled one; wall-clock used as mutation order. I2 host js_text recurses over a nested refresh_token array (≈80 KB response, 40k deep) on a Tokio worker stack → overflow; source-established, not executed. I3 AuthRequest/HttpRequest derive Debug and now carry the plaintext refresh token (no production log formats them today). Minors: MemoryLock keeps every credential id forever; publication warnings use `credential_id` which the logger drops (needs `credentialId`), scheduler failure logs lack provider/credential context; extra concurrent responses' headers unchecked; lock trait has no TTL for the future Redis adapter. Black-box assessment: scenario 5 would still pass with the in-flight map removed (MemoryLock + version re-read dedupe) — needs a simultaneous rejection/transient case; scenario 2 does not prove the one-retry guard (unit test does); scenario 4 does not prove immediate disable publication. Reviewer ran 294 tests (PG-gated tests early-returned: no live PG), clippy, OAuth 6/6, anthropic 30/30; rust-oauth binary is from dfe62cd8, one test-only commit behind HEAD.

I1 must keep snapshot reads lock-free on the request path (generation/CAS on the writer side only). Codec minor 2 stays a measured-later follow-up. Cost if wrong: time.

CLI review part 2 (ses_e2afec0ccffe89hgDhyy4SuR7x, e1a-review.md, snapshot d6294555): request changes, no Critical. I1 status.rs:49 shells out to PATH `kill -0`; spawn failure counts as "dead" and status then DELETES the pidfile of a live gateway (repro with PATH=/nonexistent; TS uses process.kill(pid,0) and keeps it). I2 password.rs enters raw mode then `eprintln!`; a stderr write failure panics, and the workspace release profile is panic=abort, so Drop never runs and the terminal stays raw (reviewer reproduced with -C panic=abort: SIGABRT, raw attributes left); the report's "restored on panic" claim is false for release builds. I3 status reads credentials/quota with its own strict SQL projection: provider_data='{' → TS exits 1 with the JSON parse error, Rust exits 0 and lists the credential healthy; quota used='bad' → TS prints "used":"bad", Rust exits 1. Minors: password-bytes.ts and status.ts matrices compare equality without asserting expected exit codes/fields (identical early failure could pass); password suite compares settings rows only, status compares no DB/pidfile state. Confirmed by reviewer: Argon2id parameters match gateway auth, cross-login test really pairs Rust CLI→TS gateway and TS CLI→Rust gateway (10 checks rerun), no password in argv/env/logs/Debug; reran password 30+2, password-bytes 17+1, cross 10, status 64+2+6, cargo test 3, clippy.
Ruling: all three are defects to fix in the CLI fix round (sent together with part 1's findings). I1: signal-zero through rustix `process` (already a dependency; no PATH lookup), with frozen semantics — TS's processAlive returns false on ANY throw, including EPERM, so a pid owned by another user counts as dead in TS too; match that and pin it with a test, and note it for the user as an inherited TS quirk. I2: every write while raw mode is active is fallible and returns through the guard; no panic path may exist between entering and leaving raw mode (release is panic=abort, so Drop-on-panic is not a guarantee); correct the report. I3: status reads through the same store/control credential and quota readers as the rest of the Rust code (TS-equal validation errors and raw scalars), not a private SQL projection. Also `systemctl` at status.rs:14 is a PATH subprocess — check what TS does and match. Cost if wrong: time.

I2 setup-limits.ts fixtures use models absent from the catalog (gpt-5, claude-sonnet-4-5) and never assert nested exit 0, so the catalog/auth/pin coverage is vacuous. Minors: M1 filesystem error text (TS `ENOTDIR: not a directory, mkdir '<path>'` vs Rust `Not a directory (os error 20)`); M2 model id `__proto__` — TS assigns into {} and the entry vanishes, Rust emits it. Ruling (snapshot publication design; replaces the CAS-retry protocol of 42a9019e): order by serialization, not by comparing rows. One writer-side mutex W covers "read the database, then publish" for the two authoritative paths — full rebuild, and credential patch (the targeted read-by-id moves INSIDE the section). Each read starts a fresh transaction inside W, so whichever section runs later has seen everything the earlier one published; the later one simply wins: a patch replaces its row with what it read (removes it if the row is gone, never inserts), a rebuild replaces the snapshot. No version/enabled/timestamp comparison, no retry loop, rebuild does exactly one read. Health patches do not take W and keep their existing rcu semantics against rebuild (as accepted in slice 1). Request-path loads never touch W. Cost: a credential patch may wait for one in-flight rebuild read (ms) — refresh is rare and already includes a network round trip. Cost if wrong: time; the regressions (R1 gated health-patch traffic during a rebuild, R2 reverse order, the three earlier interleavings, delete-vs-patch) are the bar, and the implementer may propose another protocol only if it passes all of them with bounded work.
Ruling (deep ownership, third recurrence of the same class): close it structurally, not per site — one helper for replacing/inserting into arbitrary-JSON maps that deep-drops the displaced value, used at every such site in omni-ir/omni-dialects/omni-providers (grep-enforced by a test or lint-style check), plus a low-stack matrix over every JSON-carrying block/delta kind × {start→delta replacement, merge over merge, input replacement} through decode → 3 stream encoders → collect → 3 buffered renders → drop.
Ruling (PG proof, applies to all tracks): tests gain OMNI_TEST_REQUIRE_PG=1 → a missing/unreachable database fails instead of skipping; live runs use it and print the server version. Follow-up for the controller: set it in the CI Rust job (F1) so CI cannot silently skip PG tests.

Ruling (suite guarantee, to keep this bounded): per-case expected-status tables for ~1,500 golden cases are not required. Sufficient guarantee per suite: (a) a pinned histogram of the frozen-TS exit codes over all cases (e.g. "exit 0: N, exit 1: M, exit 2: K") asserted exactly, and (b) the suite fails if any TS stderr matches a configuration/environment error it does not expect. A suite that still passes under an injected disturbance reaching both CLIs is not done. Cost if wrong: a case could swap one failure for another with the same exit code on both sides — equality of full stderr bytes already covers that.

  CONCERN reported by the implementer itself: during a slow export another admin GET takes 55–144 ms on Rust against 0.48–0.72 ms on TS; an export page holds the control connection 51–169 ms. Two orders of magnitude worse than TS for every admin request while any export is open.
Ruling (A7 export contention): defect, to be fixed before A7 is approved — not a documented difference. Export pages must not run on the shared control connection: a dedicated read-only SQLite connection per export (or from a small reader pool), a read transaction per page not held between pages, so admin latency during an export is within a small factor of the idle latency. Also find why a 500-row page costs 51–169 ms at all (30,000 rows ≈ 60 pages). Sent with the review findings as one fix round.

Refresh round-2 re-review (ses_e2b0c6939ffetLQw5cpBmyX3vd, b1-refresh-r2-review.md, 173cc9ab..c04e10f9): changes required, one Important, no Critical. ADDRESSED: R2 (stale disable cannot undo an admin re-enable; rows are re-read under W, no comparison), the three earlier interleavings, request-path load unchanged, boxing (custom path unboxed; 5,496 B confirmed by the reviewer's own run), live-PG evidence (log contains `PostgreSQL 17.11 … Alpine` from SELECT version()), four-response rejection assertions, c04e10f9 is a test-only fixture correction, rust-r3 provenance (hash verified twice). NOT fully addressed — R3: patch_credential holds W while `ArcSwap::rcu` retries (unbounded CAS loop in arc-swap 1.9.4); continuous health publications can make it lose every CAS, and while it holds W no rebuild can publish → admin changes invisible. Same starvation class as R1, moved inside the credential section. Source/dependency-level schedule, not stress-run. Minors: publication jobs spawned on the blocking pool outlive a cancelled request and are not tracked by Refresher::drain. Reviewer ran 368 tests, clippy, OAuth 7/7, 30/30.
Ruling (publication without retries): add a second, short writer-side mutex P that serialises the load → clone → modify → store step of ALL three publishers (health patch, credential patch, rebuild's store). Lock order W → P; health takes only P. Inside P use load_full + store, no rcu, so no publisher can lose a race and retry: each waits at most for the others' single clone. W is still held across read+publish for the two authoritative paths. Request-path loads touch neither mutex. P is a std mutex held for one snapshot clone — the same clone the health path already performs today, minus the wasted retries under contention. Accepted semantics unchanged: a rebuild may overwrite health published after its database read. Cost if wrong: a health publication on a request task can wait behind one other clone (µs–ms for large snapshots); under contention this is less work than the rcu retries it replaces.

- Open, raised by the implementer: (1) M2 — hyper 1.x never sends `transfer-encoding` on a HEAD response and drops one set by hand; pinned as 8 expected differences. (2) A permit is held for the whole life of an export; TS has no bound, so a fifth export visibly waits on Rust, and a client that stops reading holds its permit indefinitely — Rust has no outbound idle timeout where Bun closes the socket after ~256 s. Four stalled export clients (a viewer may export) would block every further export. (3) One WAL-checkpoint assertion failed in the first full driver run and passed on retry.
Ruling (M2, HEAD export header): accepted as a documented difference. A HEAD response has no body; the header only describes what a GET would do; no client in the repository issues HEAD on the export (dashboard and CLI use GET). Matching it would need replacing hyper's HTTP/1 response encoder. Cost if wrong: a client that inspects HEAD framing headers sees one header missing. Goes in the known-differences list for the user.
Ruling (export permits): the lifetime-of-export permit is rejected — it trades the contention defect for a starvation defect. Permit and reader connection are taken PER PAGE from a pool of four read-only connections and returned before the page is handed to the socket; no transaction between pages (already so). Then a stalled or slow client holds only its socket and one encoded page, a fifth export proceeds interleaved with the others, and no client-visible bound differs from TS. The mutant tests are reworded accordingly (more than four exports progress concurrently; at most four reader connections exist; a stalled client blocks nobody).
Ruling (WAL assertion): the property is "no export pins the WAL between pages", so the test asserts a TRUNCATE checkpoint succeeds within a bounded number of retries while the export is open, not on the first attempt.
New roadmap row A14: outbound idle timeout — a response whose client stops reading is never closed by Rust (Bun: idleTimeout 255 s). Pre-existing server behaviour, not introduced by A7; affects descriptors, not correctness. Separate row so it is measured and fixed for all response kinds at once.

Inherited TS behaviour now pinned by a fixture (for the user's list): when the id at an export page boundary is invalid UTF-8, TS binds the lossily decoded string (U+FFFD) into the next page's query; if that sorts above the stored bytes the boundary row is exported twice — 502 rows from 501 stored. Rust reproduces this byte for byte and adds no deduplication. Only reachable with ids that are not valid UTF-8 (the gateway writes UUID-style ids), so parity is kept rather than a divergence introduced.

A4 stopped on a conflict with the brief, correctly: frozen TS resolves equal-timestamp snapshots by directory enumeration order (listSnapshots: readdir → stable sort by createdAt desc; applyRetention keeps index 0 and the first preRestore) — manual-first keeps both tied files, preRestore-first keeps only preRestore. My requirement "independent of directory listing order" contradicted the reference; controller read the frozen code and confirms the implementer's reading.
Ruling (A4, controller's requirement withdrawn): Rust mirrors TS — entries in read_dir order, stable sort by createdAt descending, same index rules; no secondary sort key. Both CLIs then resolve a tie the same way on the same directory. Test: both creation orders, fixtures created identically for the two CLIs on the same filesystem, TS vs Rust equal; the dependence on enumeration order is recorded as inherited TS behaviour (a tie needs a manual and a preRestore snapshot in the same millisecond). Not hardened in Rust: a Rust-only tie-break would change which file survives.

Benchmark harness review (ses_e25d126c8ffeuyWh34Qy0iMCsG, bench-matrix-review.md, d8d8f869..c6db2950): answer to "can a TS-vs-Rust measurement from this harness be reported" = NOT YET. Sound: fairness (shared setup/load/CPU sets/order; sampled PID is the gateway itself for both — the Rust launcher execs; same /proc sampling), default path (scenario block byte-identical; ten names and JSON keys unchanged; legacy stub bytes unchanged without --matrix), runner fail-fast, namespace/OAuth seed (pinning survives the namespace; trust via environment only; seed format matches). NOT sound:
- CRITICAL: response shape is validated on the single smoke request only; the measured load is judged by HTTP status and request-log state=done. Reproduced on BOTH gateways: an upstream that answers the first request correctly and every later one with an empty 200 stream yields a "supported" scenario with metrics; on TS also with an error frame inside the stream, and with upstream 500 on a streaming ingress (downstream stays 200).
- IMPORTANT: a scenario classified failed during load still carries metrics in the JSON/markdown; comparison counts a single observation per side as better/worse and lets missing observations in; only binary hashes are checked for comparability, not duration/CPU sets/harness commit/environment.
- Disclosures needed: Rust alone runs with MIMALLOC_PURGE_DELAY=1 (its production launcher); CPU sampling ends before the final writer drain; RSS of later scenarios includes earlier ones (one process serves all); capacity check ran at concurrency 64 for the long-stream shape where gateway runs use 16; load guard checked once; refresh count not asserted zero; Responses stub omits some lifecycle events; chunks-2000 smoke accepts one delta.
Consequence: the functional claim relayed to the user earlier ("a broken upstream is reported failed, not fast") holds only for failures visible in the smoke request or as HTTP errors — not for traffic that degrades after the smoke. No measurement is run on this harness until fixed and re-reviewed.
Ruling (bench fix round 1): (1) the measured interval is validated, not only the smoke: every scenario must fail, with null metrics, under each of the reviewer's three reproductions on both gateways; required mechanisms: request-log rows for the interval all status 200 with the expected usage figures, count equal to the load generator's successes; response size per request from the load generator within the expected band for the scenario; and a verifying client running through the measured interval that reads full bodies and checks complete aggregate text, terminator, usage and absence of error frames. (2) failed → metrics null, diagnostics separate. (3) comparison: directional label only with ≥ 3 successful rounds per side, otherwise "insufficient rounds"; absent observations flagged; refusal on differing duration, warm-up, CPU sets, concurrency, harness commit or recorded environment. (4) matrix mode reports CPU both to end of load and through writer drain, same rule for both gateways; allocator/environment settings recorded per round and printed in the comparison header. (5) capacity check at each scenario's own concurrency; load guard before every round; completion manifest; refuse a non-empty OUT; refresh count asserted zero; Responses stub emits the full lifecycle; smoke checks the complete aggregate. Then re-review by the same reviewer.
The benchmark window is therefore not imminent: the hold on B3 step 3 is lifted (dispatched), since holding it no longer buys a measurement.

CLI db review (ses_e25c4cff6ffe4ZOLxzdO05rDJO, e1-db-review.md, a097828d..4ef17d62): REQUEST CHANGES. Reviewer ran: 28 unit tests, clippy, four suites (24/4/10/27+6+4), all four fail under OMNI_PORT=invalid, own probes.
- C1 (Rust-only data loss): clear-bodies joins the body root with a row's rel_path using Path::join, which REPLACES the base for an absolute path; Node path.join does not. A request_bodies row with an absolute rel_path makes Rust delete that outside file; TS leaves it. Reproduced (probe.py). Needs write access to the database to plant, but it is Rust-only destruction.
- I1: unreadable pid file (chmod 000) → TS stops with EACCES, Rust treats it as absent and proceeds to restore. Fail-open in front of the swap.
- I2: frozen nodeFs adapter swallows ALL readdir/stat failures; Rust propagates them: backup with snapshots dir 0300 → TS succeeds, Rust exits 1 after writing a complete snapshot; restore under chmod 000 / self-referential symlink / 300-char id → TS `no such snapshot`, Rust raw EACCES/ELOOP/ENAMETOOLONG.
- I3: refusal-state assertions in db-vacuum / db-backup (bad-key branch) / db-clear-bodies do not cover sidecars and snapshots; the "byte-identical on refusal" guarantee relayed earlier was only established for the restore suite.
- Minors: EISDIR message form for a snapshot-shaped directory (TS rm semantics); low-space diagnostic lacks `CONFLICT: ` and the Rust test pins the wrong string; a DELETE-mode live database is converted to WAL by merely opening it (both CLIs; header bytes 98–99 differ by SQLite version 3.53.4 vs 3.54.1) — so "nothing written on refusal" is false in TS too and must not be claimed universally.
- Passed: guard (point 4), date.rs move is byte-identical (7), no unsafe / deps chrono+rustix justified (9), pruning cannot escape the snapshots directory, id grammar, read-only candidate open, non-empty WAL probes (no extra files beyond TS).
Inherited TS hazards found by the reviewer, for the user's list: (a) clear-bodies deletes outside the body root for row paths `../outside` or through a symlinked directory — in BOTH CLIs; (b) the frozen swap copies only the candidate's main file, so rows that exist only in a candidate's WAL are counted by the preview but lost by the restore — to be demonstrated in the swap increment; (c) snapshot cleanup is not panic-safe in Rust (no reachable panic shown); SIGKILL mid-backup leaves a partial snapshot + journal in both.

Open question it raised, correctly: my ruling "refuse when the recorded environment differs within a side or between sides" cannot work as written — every round has its own scratch root/DB/cert paths, and TS and Rust differ by design (Rust's launcher sets MIMALLOC_PURGE_DELAY=1).
Ruling (bench environment comparability, replaces that clause): WITHIN a side, rounds must have the same environment after replacing the round's own scratch-root prefix in values by a placeholder (only that prefix; nothing else normalised) — a remaining difference refuses the comparison and names the variable. BETWEEN sides, differences do not refuse: they are printed in the comparison header as "environment differences between sides" (variable, base value, head value), always, because a TS-vs-Rust comparison differs by design and a base-vs-head comparison of one gateway should show at a glance if it was confounded; a `--require-same-env` option refuses instead, for base/head use.

I2 NOT fixed, reproduced only (e55be7ae, 18 failures per route kept RED): the frozen outcome depends on which pairs the JS engine's sort happens to compare — rows (0,b,BLOB label),(1,a,x),(2,b,x) succeed in Bun (the BLOB row is only ever the ARGUMENT of localeCompare, which is coerced), while the same rows with the BLOB on row 2 throw (it becomes the receiver). Implementer stopped as instructed. Workspace 1158 / 11 ignored, clippy, fmt. Closure drivers pending. Rust still logs a (sanitised) line where TS logs nothing — logs are not a parity surface; noted.
Controller probe (Bun 1.4.4 pair order for small arrays) confirms the order is an engine-internal insertion/merge scheme, deterministic but not derivable from the data alone.
Ruling (A8 I2, documented difference — the user may overturn it): Rust does NOT reproduce the JS engine's sort internals. Rule: the request fails with the same 500 as TS if and only if SOME ordered pair of distinct rows would make the frozen comparator throw (receiver's provider not a string; or providers compare equal and receiver's label not a string; or both equal and receiver's windowType not a string) — independent of sort algorithm. Consequences, all provable: (1) singleton/empty never fail — equals TS; (2) malformed later fields behind differing earlier fields never fail — equals TS; (3) if TS answers 500 then Rust answers 500 (TS can only throw on a pair that exists); (4) if Rust answers 200 the comparator is total and both sorts are stable, so the bytes equal TS; (5) the only difference: arrangements where a throwing pair exists but the engine's sort happens never to evaluate it — TS 200, Rust 500. Reachable only with hand-edited credential/quota cells (TS writes these columns as strings). Reason for not porting the engine's sort: a hand-copied engine algorithm pinned to one Bun build, for an outcome that in TS itself is an accident of row order. Cost if wrong: an operator with such a tampered row sees the client quota routes fail in Rust for row orders where TS would have answered.

Controller probe (scratch, removed): Bun 1.4.4 `CompressionStream("zstd",{level:3})` vs `zstd::stream::write::Encoder::new(_, 3)` + write_all + finish, for both crate versions: BYTE-IDENTICAL frames on three inputs — 7-byte JSON (16 bytes), a 62 kB highly compressible body (352 bytes), a 507 kB mostly incompressible multi-block body (305,532 bytes); frame header 28b52ffd 00 58… (no content size, not single-segment, window descriptor 0x58 = 2 MiB) as the spec says; system zstd 1.5.7 decodes to the original. Bench harness re-review (ses_e25d126c8ffeuyWh34Qy0iMCsG, bench-matrix-rereview.md, c6db2950..9108593a = 10 commits, 26 files, scripts/ + docs/benchmarks/ only): answer = YES WITH STATED CAVEATS. Dispositions: Critical PARTIALLY (three mechanisms implemented; gross failures fail; same-size semantic corruption of load responses is not detectable — one wrong same-width character in an unverified load response passes; even 100 % load-key corruption passes if the verifier's own replies stay correct; no guaranteed detectable fraction), failed-metrics ADDRESSED, rounds ADDRESSED, incompatible experiments PARTIALLY.
New/residual: IMPORTANT (a) comparison matches scenarios by name only — with one process serving scenarios in order, runs with different selections or orders have different process/DB history and are still compared; IMPORTANT (b) environment masking is by variable NAME only — a credential inside a URL value (e.g. a database URL with userinfo) is recorded and printed verbatim; (c) bench-capacity.ts never gates the single-client capacity files (synthetic 500-only c1 files pass); (d) verifier sleeps 250 ms AFTER each response, so its rate depends on latency — the faster gateway does more verifier work; (e) minors: scratch-root normalisation is substring-wide, not prefix; comparison header lacks CPU scope, memory history, the 250 ms tolerance and the verifier's cost.
Ruling (bench fix round 2, before any measurement — all small): (a) comparison refuses unless both sides and all rounds have the same ORDERED scenario selection (or all were taken with --fresh-process, which is then stated); (b) recorded values: URL userinfo and credential-like query parameters redacted in any value, in addition to name-based masking, with tests; (c) capacity checker gates every concurrency it probed and fails on a file it does not recognise; (d) verifier on a fixed schedule (one request started every 250 ms regardless of response time, at most one in flight; skipped ticks counted) so both sides receive the same verifier load; (e) both minors. NOT changed: the same-size-corruption blind spot — accepted as a stated limit ("integrity of the measured traffic is checked by status, usage, per-request size and a sampling full-body verifier; it is not a proof that every response was correct"). Reason: neither gateway is adversarial toward the harness; the failure classes that make a gateway look fast (errors, truncation, empty or partial streams, missing usage, dropped log rows) all change status, size or usage. Cost if wrong: a gateway bug that corrupts text at equal length under load only would go unseen by the benchmark — it is the conformance drivers' job, not the benchmark's.

- R1 (Important, conservative — Rust refuses MORE than TS, no bypass): frozen service.ts:143 calls existsSync before reading the pid file. - Swap notes: re-validate the candidate after confirmation under the guard; a swallowed stat is not proof of absence — never use it as authority to overwrite or delete.

I1 ADDRESSED for provider_data, I3 ADDRESSED, I2 PARTIALLY. The I2 ruling itself judged reasonable as an explicit malformed-data divergence. New:
- R1 (Important, normal schema): a credential whose id is a BLOB. TS joins quota rows to credentials through a JS Map keyed by the cell value; two separately read Uint8Arrays are different keys, so the row never joins and TS OMITS it; Rust joins BLOBs by byte equality and DISCLOSES the account/sample. Rust shows more than TS — the wrong direction on a disclosure route — and breaks "Rust 200 ⇒ bytes equal".
- R2 (Important, hand-altered schema only): history's fourth sort key is numeric subtraction; with non-numeric observedAt (reachable only if quota_samples.observed_at is given TEXT affinity by hand) the comparator is not a total order: 5-row case TS and Rust both 200 in different orders; 22-row case TS 200, Rust's sort PANICS ("does not correctly implement a total order"), caught → 500; no process crash.
- R3 (Important, pre-existing from the key-auth layer): /api/client/summary with a hand-broken api_keys.model_allowlist — both 500 generic, Rust logs the parser message quoting the stored value (admin_password.rs:146–149 → api.rs:160).
- Notes: the sanitised metadata log line contains the credential id (deliberate; ids are operator-visible identifiers — accepted, report wording to be exact); the unit test's brute force reuses production sort_keys/locale_compare (verifies grouping, not JS coercion); a 50,002-row history request took 1.6 s on a DEBUG Rust build vs 0.1 s TS — not a release measurement, but O(samples × credentials) account lookup is noted → controller to time it on a release build before A8 is ticked.
Ruling (A8 fix round 2): R1 — join with JS key semantics: text ids by value, numeric ids as JS numbers, BLOB ids never match (identity); both routes, and audit every other place in the client and operator routes where Rust joins rows by a cell that could be a BLOB against a JS Map/Set/=== in TS; say what is found. R2 — no panic is possible on a request path, whatever the database holds: every comparator handed to a sort is a total order (prove or make it so, also the quota route's); for non-numeric observedAt Rust orders by the three string keys, then numeric observedAt with non-numeric values after all numeric ones in input order — documented difference "history order with non-numeric observedAt (requires a hand-altered column affinity)", one pinned case; not a port of the engine's behaviour on a non-total comparator. R3 — stored-value parse failures never carry parser text into logs: fix at every site where a stored JSON column's parse error is turned into a logged message (key rows, credential rows, settings, anything else found by grep), fixed reason + row id only; canary tests on the log for each site found.

Controller design note for R3 (suggestion to the implementer, to avoid site-by-site message editing): the leak is structural — free-form parser text travels inside StoreError::Corrupt(String) / Result<_, String> and is printed by `%e` at the internal-error log sinks. Carry stored-JSON parse failures as a distinct error variant with table, column, row id and the parser detail as a separate field; Display (what the sinks log) prints only the fixed part; the few wire consumers that must send the frozen text read the detail field explicitly. Then the audit is "every producer of that parser text for a stored cell uses the variant" + "every log sink prints Display only".

- NEW Important (regression from round 2's verifier change): scripts/bench-verify-client.ts:63 uses `process.once("SIGTERM", …)`; the harness signals the verifier at the load boundary (bench-gateway.ts:1147) and again after the drain (1201). If a verifier request is still in flight at the second signal, the handler is gone, the process dies without reporting, and the harness throws on empty stdout — the run aborts with no JSON. Reproduced on both gateways with custom-chat.chunks-2000 behind an upstream delayed by 600 ms. It aborts loudly (no wrong number can result), but a real measurement with slow long streams would die mid-run.

Controller hypothesis for the I3 mismatch (unverified; passed to the implementer as a hypothesis): both of those cases WRITE a new snapshot. BLOCKER found by the controller before starting the rounds: scripts/bench-rounds.sh checks the 1-minute load before EVERY side invocation and REFUSES (exit 2) when it is above MAX_LOAD — it does not wait. A matrix run keeps eight cores busy for minutes, so right after base/1 the load average is far above 1 and head/1 would be refused: with the guard enabled, a real multi-round run cannot get past its first side. Never seen before because every functional multi-round run by implementers and the reviewer used ALLOW_BUSY=1 (shared host), and the reviewer tested the per-round guard with a shimmed load value. Not a correctness risk (it aborts), but the guard as built is unusable for its purpose. Fix (small, harness only): before each invocation WAIT for the load to fall to the threshold (poll, bounded by LOAD_WAIT seconds, default 600), record the wait and the load at start in the round's metadata, refuse only on timeout; plus one real two-round run WITHOUT ALLOW_BUSY as the test. Dispatched to the bench implementer session; the other tracks stay held.

A8 re-review 2 (ses_e25b95abcffeLxwgNT2yKwYUoj, a8-rereview2.md; fresh build cc8adc29… from 20be5c97; ran: 9 stock client configurations 17,609 equal / 14 expected / 0 failures, surface 620 / 61 / 0, own key-semantics, adversarial 8,192-row, canary and inference-log probes; did not run workspace tests or clippy): VERDICT request changes. R1 ADDRESSED. R3 ADDRESSED (detail unreachable through Display/Debug/source/conversions; inference inheritance re-observed on all three paths in the frozen log). R2 PARTIALLY: comparators total, no panic; but N1 (Important) — the history route's preliminary sort (client_quota.rs:85–90, 181–184) puts every numeric cell before every string cell, while the frozen store sort (packages/store/src/sqlite/credentials.ts:530–536) uses JS `<`/`>` which coerces mixed operands numerically. With a no-affinity table holding credentials id=2 and id='1' and equal numeric observed_at, TS answers credentialId order ["1",2], Rust [2,"1"]; at the 50,001-row trim the differing order can also change which row is cut (not probed). Not in the report's known differences. Minors: (a) the log rule's wording must keep the stated exception (sanitised lines carry stored ids); (b) malformed api_keys.limits makes TS log `api key limits unreadable apiKeyId=…` on client reads and Rust logs nothing there (36 equal wire comparisons, one log comparison differs; no leak).
Ruling: N1 is the same class as the R2 decision and is resolved the same way. JS relational comparison over mixed number/string cells is not a total order (e.g. "10" < "9" as strings, "9" < 9.5 and 9.5 < "10" numerically), so the frozen result for such data depends on the engine's sort internals, which are deliberately not ported. Rust keeps its total order (numeric cells first, then strings in UTF-16 order). The documented difference is widened from "non-numeric observedAt" to "history order, and which row the 50,001-row trim cuts, when credential_id / window_type / observed_at cells have mixed or non-declared types — reachable only with a hand-altered column affinity (the schema declares TEXT/TEXT/INTEGER)". The reviewer's concrete exchange is pinned in the driver with both sides asserted in full.
Ruling: minor (b) is fixed, not documented, if the place is inside the A8 files: the frozen gateway tells the operator about an unreadable limits cell whenever the key row is read, a fixed message with the key id and no stored content; Rust should say the same in the same situation. BENCH DIAGNOSIS (ses_e232bf91cffeVffuYzJ4ukG6PA, diag-bench-findings.md, investigation only; scratch reverted, evidence /tmp/opencode/diag/):
Finding 1 — CONFIRMED product defect, and the controller's hypothesis about the mechanism was WRONG in its detail. Counts (upstream sockets accepted / requests): Rust Anthropic path 1,030 / 1,030 sequential and 1,028 / 1,028 at 32 clients, zero pool check-ins; Rust custom path 2 / 1,028 and 32 / 1,028; frozen TS Anthropic path 2 / 1,030 and 31 / 1,028. Cause: the Anthropic decoder emits `End` at `message_stop` but returns `Flow::Continue` (crates/omni-providers/src/anthropic/decode.rs:303–311, 344); dispatch breaks on End (crates/omni-gateway/src/dispatch.rs:843–844) and drops the event stream with the body still owned, so the connection is never released. The custom decoders return `Flow::Stop`, which calls `finish_if_ready()`. Confirmed by a scratch change (stop at message_stop): 2 / 1,028 and 32 / 1,028. Neither `still_idle` nor TLS session tickets is involved (the stub answers HTTP/1.1 with Content-Length, no Connection header). A second, separate limitation also confirmed: with a chunked response whose terminator arrives 20 ms late, `finish_if_ready()` never waits → 516 / 516 even with the decoder change. Real api.anthropic.com streams chunked, so the second one matters outside the bench. Token refresh reads bodies to completion and is not affected (source reading, not counted). NOT established: that this explains the measured tail/plateau/RSS (to be re-measured after the fix).
Finding 2 — policy confirmed, the particular stall NOT identified. The writer queue is a 131,072-event bounded channel with try_send; a full queue drops the incoming event, counts it, warns rate-limited and at shutdown (crates/omni-store/src/writer/task.rs:118–124, 353–390, 246–265). Reproduced deterministically at capacity 4 (10 sends → 6 dropped) and not at capacity 10. What stalled the writer for > ~1.2 s in head round 3 cannot be told from the retained evidence (no queue depth, batch duration or checkpoint trace). The frozen gateway writes synchronously on its request path: it never drops, it slows down.
Controller reading of the spec for finding 2: this is the APPROVED design, not a defect — spec "All queues are bounded … when a queue is full, the request path does not wait; it sheds" and "Overload: if the writer's channel is full, the request is still served and its log event is counted as dropped, with a metric and a rate-limited warn … This is the builder's call: shedding traffic instead is defensible. Flag it in review if you disagree." The diagnosis agent's proposal (await queue capacity = back-pressure) contradicts the spec.
Ruling: the drop policy stays as specified; changing it to back-pressure is the USER's decision and is put to them as an open question (the measured fact that makes it worth asking: 1.2 % of rows lost once in three runs at ~55k req/s with a healthy disk, where the frozen gateway loses none). What is done without asking: writer diagnostics so the next incident can be explained (queue high-water mark, longest batch, longest checkpoint, reported in the drop warning / shutdown line / metrics) — separate small task after the connection fix; and the rerun watches for recurrence.
Ruling: finding 1 is fixed in two separately committed steps in worktree /tmp/opencode/bench-gate/src, branch fix/upstream-conn-reuse (from main ef46adb6): (1) the stream's body is finished when the terminal event is produced, for EVERY provider decoder (generic, not an Anthropic special case), with a regression test that counts accepted upstream sockets through the real dispatch path; (2) a bounded completion drain for a terminator that has not arrived yet — owned by the pool, bounded in bytes, time and number, never on the request's own path, closed on cancel/error/timeout; the frozen gateway's behaviour for the delayed terminator is observed first. Step 2 is reviewed on its own and may be rejected without losing step 1.

Ruling (USER, Nov 15): request-log writer overload policy stays as the spec has it — when the writer's queue is full the request is served and its log event is dropped, counted, warned ("tetap buang untuk sekarang"). Ruling: step 2 (bounded completion drain for a terminator that arrives after the terminal event) WILL be implemented — a provider that streams chunked would otherwise still lose the connection per request, and the bench stub (Content-Length) cannot show it. Design accepted as proposed with these constraints: pool-owned, never on the request's path; bounds 64 KiB / 100 ms / 32 concurrent, over capacity → close; any error, timeout, extra bytes or malformed framing → close; client cancel before End closes, after hand-off does not cancel the drain; drains stop at shutdown. The frozen gateway's count for the delayed-terminator fixture is observed FIRST. Order: review of step 1 → step 2 in a fresh session → its own review → merge → matrix rerun.

Item B (zstd request body in the HTTP client) NOT started — the session ran out of context after the reading. Its reading of the frozen transport for an empty body (code only): http-client.ts 179–181 compresses unconditionally; 99–100 appends Content-Length only when the SOURCE body is non-empty; 154 writes the compressed bytes when non-empty → an empty source body goes out as a 9-byte zstd frame WITHOUT an explicit Content-Length (so presumably chunked by node:https — to be observed on the wire, not assumed). OAuth requests always carry a JSON body, so this is an edge, but it decides what "equal headers" means there.
Dispatched: fresh session for item B only.
B3 zstd request body (ses_e22e8f380ffezAmJ8b3VHjabQ4) at f35643f0, worktree clean; touched only http/mod.rs, http/wire.rs, tests, docs (controller read the diff). Failed first: UnsupportedEncoding(Zstd) (s5b-red.log). Client now compresses a non-empty encoded body at level 3 (one write, finish), Content-Length = compressed length appended where the frozen transport appends it; refuses before any network activity an encoded request carrying a content-length or not exactly one `content-encoding: zstd` (frozen text kept). Compared with the frozen transport through a reference script, five probes incl. a real OAuth codec request and a > 3 MiB body: header order equal, decompressed body equal, and compressed bytes IDENTICAL in all five. Controller checked logs: 53 ok summaries / no FAILED (implementer: 235 passed, 4 ignored); clippy Finished, 0 errors; fmt "exit code: 0".
Empty body, OBSERVED on the frozen transport (controller decoded s5b-empty.log): `POST / HTTP/1.1 | content-encoding: zstd | Host | Connection: keep-alive | Transfer-Encoding: chunked`, then one 9-byte chunk 28 b5 2f fd 00 58 01 00 00 and the terminator — i.e. no Content-Length, chunked. Rust refuses with HttpError::EmptyEncodedBody.
Ruling: the refusal STAYS (documented difference). Reasons: the only producer of encoded requests is the OAuth codec, whose body is always a non-empty JSON object, so the case is unreachable from the gateway; reproducing it needs a chunked-upload branch plus a frame whose header bytes differ from Rust's encoder for the empty input (known since the encoder probe); a refusal before any network activity is the safe failure. If a later provider needs encoded empty bodies, this is reopened.

DRAIN step 2 (ses_e22ec263cffeWsTKCT9Qj4qdC7) at e441bdc2 on fix/upstream-conn-reuse, worktree clean; controller checked logs: 60 ok summaries / 321 passed / 5 ignored / no FAILED; 20/20 loop runs; clippy Finished; fmt exit=0.
Step 0 CHANGES THE BASIS of the controller's earlier ruling: for a chunked streaming response whose terminator arrives in a later write, the FROZEN gateway does not reuse the connection either — 20 sockets / 20 requests for delays 0 ms, 20 ms, 200 ms, 2 s and never (custom chat-completions credential, raw TCP origin; Rust at 21ccf9be the same; no incomplete socket reused by either; the baseline binary fix-21ccf9be was built by the controller from the clean worktree at that commit). So the drain is not parity work: it makes Rust reuse upstream connections where the frozen gateway opens a new one per streaming request. Consequence for the benchmark too: the stub answers with Content-Length, so BOTH gateways' Anthropic-path numbers assume reuse that neither would get from a provider that streams chunked.
Implemented: pool-owned JoinSet, ≤ 32 drains, 64 KiB wire budget, 100 ms deadline; closes on overflow, timeout, malformed framing, read error, early EOF, excess buffered bytes, no runtime, pool shutdown. Tests: delayed terminator 1/20 (failed first), custom Responses 1/20, five waves of 32 at 32 sockets, forged response after hand-off rejected, drain unit tests. Missing from the brief: never-terminator with next-request isolation, EOF with the completed client unaffected, joined shutdown, no-runtime case.
DEFECT in the change (implementer's own concern, controller confirms): to keep the dispatch future under its 5,500-byte gate, `next_chunk` now does `Box::pin(self.read_next())` — a heap allocation per body read on EVERY stream. The spec says "There is no per-chunk allocation beyond the encoded frame". Controller's quick bench (release, 2 runs each, 6 s, host busy with foreign load 0.3–3.8 cores, so weak): gateway CPU per request on the 2,000-chunk scenarios 21ccf9be → e441bdc2: custom chat 1.55/1.64 → 1.61/1.66; custom Responses 2.94/2.99 → 3.06/3.10; Anthropic 2.03/1.95 → 2.10/2.07 ms — about +3–5 % in two of three, inside noise for the third. Not proof, but the spec rule decides it.
Ruling: the hot-path boxing is removed (the drain's own future may be boxed — it runs once per response, off the request path); the four missing tests are added; then a separate review. Ruling on whether to keep the drain at all, given step 0: KEEP, as a documented Rust-only difference ("Rust returns an upstream connection to the pool after a chunked streaming response when its terminator arrives within 100 ms / 64 KiB; the frozen gateway closes it") — it serves the user's goal (a TLS handshake saved per streaming request against real providers) and every reuse still passes the unchanged framing and idle checks the reviewer probed. Put to the user as theirs to veto; merged only after its review approves.

B3 request-side review (ses_e22de8741ffea2BRr08a6eV9cb, b3-request-review.md, f35643f0; own corpus 161 whole requests, 11 differing; gate rerun 53 summaries / 235 passed / 4 ignored / no FAILED, clippy Finished): REQUEST CHANGES. Important 1: an own `__proto__` key in the client's vendor.openai bag on the OAuth path — the frozen builder's assignment invokes the prototype setter, so the key is not serialised and values under it are seen by later inherited lookups (routing hint gains `tier=priority`); Rust serialises `"__proto__":{…}` and derives no tier (4 rows). Important 2: native history items with absent fields (function_call name/arguments/call_id; function_call_output call_id/output; message content) — frozen omits them, Rust writes explicit nulls (6 rows; lite.rs:147,161,169,177 index into the map). Minor 3: the transport's content-encoding check uses Rust `trim`, the frozen adapter JS `trim` (U+0085 accepted by Rust, refused by frozen; U+FEFF the other way).
